(2011](#right-ref-B143), p. 976) concluded that their results were “consistent with the hypothesis that, as a result of neural sensitization in vulnerable individuals, reward-related cues are attributed with pathological incentive salience, leading to compulsive pursuit.” Further, the amount of L-Dopa-evoked dopamine release in patients with DDS was “correlated with self-reported compulsive drug wanting but not liking, and was related to heightened psychomotor activation (punding)” ([Evans et al. 2006](#right-ref-B57), p. 852), consistent with IST.

6.4.  The Role of Uncertainty

Perhaps especially relevant to gambling addiction, uncertainty about whether a reward will be gained after a reward cue can magnify the incentive salience attributed to that cue and magnify the cue-triggered activation of mesolimbic dopamine systems—even though uncertainty reduces the predictive reliability of those cues ([Fiorillo et al. 2003](#right-ref-B65), [Robinson & Anselme 2019](#right-ref-B157), [Singer et al. 2020](#right-ref-B184)). For example, an uncertain cue that predicts a reward on 50% of occasions becomes more attractive, eliciting stronger sign-tracking in rats, than a cue that predicts a reward 100% of the time ([Anselme et al. 2013](#right-ref-B8), [Robinson et al. 2014a](#right-ref-B158)). In addition, uncertainty about cues and a food reward sensitizes the subsequent psychomotor response to amphetamine ([Singer et al. 2012](#right-ref-B186), [Zeeb et al. 2017](#right-ref-B231), [Zack et al. 2020](#right-ref-B230)). Cue-reward uncertainty increases amphetamine self-administration and food seeking by animals ([Mascia et al. 2019](#right-ref-B136), [Robinson et al. 2023](#right-ref-B160)), magnifies amphetamine-induced mesolimbic sensitization ([Robinson et al. 2015](#right-ref-B159)), and alters mesocorticolimbic dopamine and glutamate systems in ways similar to that induced by intermittent exposure to drugs ([Mascia et al. 2019](#right-ref-B136), [2020](#right-ref-B137)). In humans, problematic gambling is associated with enhanced dopamine release elicited by amphetamine ([Boileau et al. 2014](#right-ref-B30)), possibly also reflecting mesolimbic sensitization. Thus, by rendering mesolimbic circuitry hyperresponsive, uncertainty can produce sensitization-like effects, including an increase in motivation for rewards and their cues.

7.  CONCLUSIONS

Looking back, we are surprised at the longevity of IST, originally proposed in 1993, given the pace of developments in the field. However, in our view the weight of additional evidence accumulated over 30 years has continued to support the major tenets of IST—namely, that mesolimbic sensitization of incentive salience leads to excessive “wanting” for drugs, and an increasing dissociation between “wanting” and liking. Although dopamine participates in many behavioral and psychological functions, we also think there is now a consensus that mesolimbic dopamine's chief role in reward is to mediate reward “wanting” but not liking. Since it was originally proposed, IST theory has expanded its scope to also include behavioral addictions, attentional capture by reward cues, medication-induced compulsions, uncertainty effects, and even the paranoia of schizophrenia ([Kapur 2003](#right-ref-B96)), among others. Of course, the precise neural circuitry mechanisms and neurochemical changes, including in dopamine systems, that mediate incentive sensitization to selectively increase “wanting” are still an active area of investigation today ([Fraser et al. 2023](#right-ref-B69)).

We acknowledge, as we always have, that there are many other factors that promote problematic drug use besides sensitization of incentive salience, including withdrawal or distress relief, social factors, and so on. Yet, in closing, we maintain now, as we did in 1993, that incentive sensitization carries the main explanatory weight in accounting for why particular individuals become compulsively addicted in the sense of “wanting” drugs so much. IST also helps explain why addicted individuals remain prone to relapse even after months or years of drug abstinence, even if not in withdrawal or other distress, and even if they do not expect to like their drugs very much. Thus, we conclude that accumulating evidence over the past 30 years has both bolstered support for the basic tenets of IST and expanded its reach.

SUMMARY POINTS

1. 1\. People take drugs of abuse for many reasons (pleasure, social conformity, distress relief, etc.), and most do not become compulsively addicted, although some individuals do.
2. 2\. Understanding addiction requires explaining how vulnerable individuals develop urges to take drugs that are so intense as to be arguably compulsive.
3. 3\. In vulnerable individuals, repeated exposure to potentially addictive drugs can sensitize mesolimbic dopamine (and related) systems, which amplifies the incentive salience attributed to drug cues and drug taking, making drugs more “wanted” (but not more liked) for years after the cessation of drug use.
4. 4\. The persistence of incentive sensitization explains why people with a substance use disorder can remain vulnerable to relapse long after the discontinuation of drug use, even in the absence of withdrawal or other distress, and even if the drug is not liked much anymore.
5. 5\. The expression of sensitization can be very context dependent, being preferentially expressed in contexts in which drugs have been experienced before, which helps explain why relapse often occurs when drug-associated cues and contexts are present.
6. 6\. States of stress, withdrawal, emotional excitement, and so on, can amplify sensitized incentive salience further, causing even more intense “wanting”, which helps explain why such states increase the probability of relapse and drug taking.
7. 7\. Excessive cue-triggered “wanting” due to incentive sensitization may also contribute to the development of so-called behavioral addictions such as compulsive gambling, shopping, eating, sex, and so on, including medication-induced addictive behavior in Parkinson's patients.
8. 8\. Although many other factors, such as habits, pleasure seeking, withdrawal avoidance, impaired executive/inhibitory control, and so on, also contribute to drug use, incentive-sensitization theory may best explain why susceptible individuals become compulsively addicted and why they remain so susceptible to relapse long after the discontinuation of drug use.

FUTURE ISSUES

1. 1\. What neural circuitry, including corticolimbic glutamate as well as mesolimbic dopaminergic circuitry, mediates incentive salience (wanting) and incentive sensitization, and how?
2. 2\. What is the relationship between psychological phenomena studied in humans, such as an attentional bias to reward cues, and related phenomena studied in preclinical studies, such as sign-tracking?
3. 3\. How do bottom-up processes, such as incentive salience attribution, interact with top-down processes, such as executive/inhibitory control, in the development of addiction?
4. 4\. How can contextual factors, which gate the ability of drug cues to trigger incentive sensitization, be better incorporated into behavioral and neuroimaging studies of addiction in humans?
5. 5\. How do the processes of incentive motivation, affect, and learning interact, at both behavioral and neurobiological levels, to produce motivated behavior?
6. 6\. How do the affective states associated with stress, excitement, withdrawal, and so on amplify sensitized incentive salience to increase cue-triggered wanting even further?
7. 7\. What are the relative roles of implicit versus explicit psychological processes in addiction?
8. 8\. What psychological and/or brain-based treatments can decrease incentive sensitization, and therefore the degree to which drugs are “wanted”, without degrading all motivated behavior?
